					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Country in Northwestern Europe</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the country in Europe. For the sovereign state of which it is a part, see <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands" title="Kingdom of the Netherlands">Kingdom of the Netherlands</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Netherlands_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Netherlands (disambiguation)">Netherlands (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Nederland" redirects here. <p>The <b>Netherlands</b> (<a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a>: <i lang="nl">Nederland</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="nl-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Dutch" title="Help:IPA/Dutch">[ˈneːdərlɑnt]</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nl-Nederland.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0a/Nl-Nederland.ogg" class="internal" title="Nl-Nederland.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span>), <a href="/wiki/Terminology_of_the_Low_Countries" title="Terminology of the Low Countries">informally</a> <b>Holland</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-:2_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-20">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Netherlands_vs._Holland_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Netherlands_vs._Holland-21">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> is a country located in <a href="/wiki/Northwestern_Europe" title="Northwestern Europe">Northwestern Europe</a> with overseas territories in the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a>. It is the largest of four constituent countries of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands" title="Kingdom of the Netherlands">Kingdom of the Netherlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The Netherlands consists of <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_the_Netherlands" title="Provinces of the Netherlands">twelve provinces</a>; it borders <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> to the east, and <a href="/wiki/Belgium" title="Belgium">Belgium</a> to the south, with a <a href="/wiki/North_Sea" title="North Sea">North Sea</a> coastline to the north and west. It shares <a href="/wiki/Maritime_boundary" title="Maritime boundary">maritime borders</a> with the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a>, Germany and Belgium in the North Sea.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The country's official language is Dutch, with <a href="/wiki/West_Frisian_language" title="West Frisian language">West Frisian</a> as a secondary official language in the province of <a href="/wiki/Friesland" title="Friesland">Friesland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-official-and-recognised-languages_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-official-and-recognised-languages-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Dutch, English and <a href="/wiki/Papiamento" title="Papiamento">Papiamento</a> are official in the Caribbean territories.<sup id="cite_ref-official-and-recognised-languages_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-official-and-recognised-languages-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The four largest cities in the Netherlands are <a href="/wiki/Amsterdam" title="Amsterdam">Amsterdam</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rotterdam" title="Rotterdam">Rotterdam</a>, <a href="/wiki/The_Hague" title="The Hague">The Hague</a> and <a href="/wiki/Utrecht" title="Utrecht">Utrecht</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Amsterdam is the country's most populous city and the nominal <a href="/wiki/Capital_of_the_Netherlands" title="Capital of the Netherlands">capital</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The Hague holds the seat of the <a href="/wiki/States_General_of_the_Netherlands" title="States General of the Netherlands">States General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_Netherlands" title="Cabinet of the Netherlands">Cabinet</a> and <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_Netherlands" title="Supreme Court of the Netherlands">Supreme Court</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Port_of_Rotterdam" title="Port of Rotterdam">Port of Rotterdam</a> is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_busiest_ports_in_Europe" title="List of busiest ports in Europe">busiest seaport in Europe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ec.europa.eu/eurostat/web/products-eurostat-news/-/ddn-20200402-2_2021-11-25_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ec.europa.eu/eurostat/web/products-eurostat-news/-/ddn-20200402-2_2021-11-25-27">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Amsterdam_Airport_Schiphol" title="Amsterdam Airport Schiphol">Schiphol</a> is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_the_Netherlands" title="List of the busiest airports in the Netherlands">busiest airport in the Netherlands</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Europe" title="List of the busiest airports in Europe">third busiest in Europe</a>. The Netherlands is a founding member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eurozone" title="Eurozone">Eurozone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Group_of_Ten_(economics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Group of Ten (economics)">G10</a>, <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a>, <a href="/wiki/OECD" title="OECD">OECD</a>, and <a href="/wiki/World_Trade_Organization" title="World Trade Organization">WTO</a>, as well as a part of the <a href="/wiki/Schengen_Area" title="Schengen Area">Schengen Area</a> and the trilateral <a href="/wiki/Benelux" title="Benelux">Benelux</a> Union. It hosts several <a href="/wiki/Intergovernmental_organization" class="mw-redirect" title="Intergovernmental organization">intergovernmental organisations</a> and <a href="/wiki/International_court" title="International court">international courts</a>, many of which are centred in The Hague.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="name"></span><i>Netherlands</i> literally means "lower countries" in reference to its low elevation and flat topography, with nearly 26% falling <a href="/wiki/Height_above_mean_sea_level" title="Height above mean sea level">below sea level</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-29">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Most of the areas below sea level, known as <a href="/wiki/Polder" title="Polder"><i>polders</i></a>, are the result of <a href="/wiki/Land_reclamation_in_the_Netherlands" title="Land reclamation in the Netherlands">land reclamation that began in the 14th century</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Cavendish_2003_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cavendish_2003-30">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Republican period</a>, which began in 1588, the Netherlands entered a unique era of political, economic, and cultural greatness, ranked among the most powerful and influential in Europe and the world; this period is known as the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Golden_Age" title="Dutch Golden Age">Dutch Golden Age</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> During this time, its trading companies, the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company" title="Dutch East India Company">Dutch East India Company</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company" title="Dutch West India Company">Dutch West India Company</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Empire" title="Dutch Empire">established colonies and trading posts</a> all over the world.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With a population of 17.7 million people, all living within a total area of 41,850&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (16,160&#160;sq&#160;mi)—of which the land area is 33,500&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (12,900&#160;sq&#160;mi)—the Netherlands is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population_density" title="List of countries and dependencies by population density">16th most densely populated</a> country in the world and the <a href="/wiki/Area_and_population_of_European_countries" title="Area and population of European countries">second-most densely populated</a> country in the European Union, with a density of 520 people per square kilometre (1,300 people/sq&#160;mi). Nevertheless, it is the world's second-largest <a href="/wiki/Export" title="Export">exporter</a> of food and agricultural products by value, owing to its <a href="/wiki/Soil_fertility" title="Soil fertility">fertile soil</a>, mild climate, <a href="/wiki/Intensive_farming" title="Intensive farming">intensive agriculture</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Dutch_inventions_and_innovations" title="List of Dutch inventions and innovations">inventiveness</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-hollandtrade.com_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hollandtrade.com-35">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nationalgeographic.com_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nationalgeographic.com-36">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Netherlands has been a <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_system" title="Parliamentary system">parliamentary</a> <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy" title="Constitutional monarchy">constitutional monarchy</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Unitary_state" title="Unitary state">unitary structure</a> since 1848. The country has a tradition of <a href="/wiki/Pillarisation" title="Pillarisation">pillarisation</a> and a long record of <a href="/wiki/Toleration" title="Toleration">social tolerance</a>, having legalised <a href="/wiki/Abortion_in_the_Netherlands" title="Abortion in the Netherlands">abortion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prostitution_in_the_Netherlands" title="Prostitution in the Netherlands">prostitution</a> and <a href="/wiki/Euthanasia_in_the_Netherlands" title="Euthanasia in the Netherlands">euthanasia</a>, along with maintaining a liberal <a href="/wiki/Drug_policy_of_the_Netherlands" title="Drug policy of the Netherlands">drug policy</a>. The Netherlands allowed <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage" title="Women&#39;s suffrage">women's suffrage</a> in 1919 and was the first country to legalise <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_Netherlands" title="Same-sex marriage in the Netherlands">same-sex marriage</a> in 2001. Its <a href="/wiki/Mixed_economy" title="Mixed economy">mixed-market</a> <a href="/wiki/Developed_country" title="Developed country">advanced economy</a> has the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)_per_capita" title="List of countries by GDP (nominal) per capita">thirteenth-highest</a> <a href="/wiki/Per_capita_income" title="Per capita income">per capita income</a> globally. The Netherlands ranks among the highest in international indices of <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press" title="Freedom of the press">press freedom</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Economic_freedom" title="Economic freedom">economic freedom</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">human development</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quality_of_life" title="Quality of life">quality of life</a>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Happiness" title="Happiness">happiness</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Terminology_of_the_Low_Countries" title="Terminology of the Low Countries">Terminology of the Low Countries</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Netherlands_and_the_Low_Countries">Netherlands and the Low Countries</span></h3>
<p>The region called the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a> (comprising <a href="/wiki/Belgium" title="Belgium">Belgium</a>, the Netherlands and <a href="/wiki/Luxembourg" title="Luxembourg">Luxembourg</a>) has the same <a href="/wiki/Toponymy" title="Toponymy">toponymy</a>. Place names with <i>Neder</i>, <i>Nieder</i>, <i>Nedre</i>, <i>Nether</i>, <i>Lage(r)</i> or <i>Low(er)</i> (in <a href="/wiki/Germanic_languages" title="Germanic languages">Germanic languages</a>) and <i>Bas</i> or <i>Inferior</i> (in <a href="/wiki/Romance_languages" title="Romance languages">Romance languages</a>) are in use in low-lying places all over Europe. In the case of the Low Countries and the Netherlands, the geographical location of the <i>lower</i> region has been more or less downstream and near the sea. The <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Romans</a> made a distinction between the Roman provinces of downstream <a href="/wiki/Germania_Inferior" title="Germania Inferior">Germania Inferior</a> (nowadays part of Belgium and the Netherlands) and upstream <a href="/wiki/Germania_Superior" title="Germania Superior">Germania Superior</a>. The designation 'Low' returned in the 10th-century Duchy of <a href="/wiki/Lower_Lorraine" class="mw-redirect" title="Lower Lorraine">Lower Lorraine</a>, which covered much of the Low Countries.<sup id="cite_ref-Franks_(Columbia_Encyclopedia)_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Franks_(Columbia_Encyclopedia)-41">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Dukes_of_Burgundy" class="mw-redirect" title="Dukes of Burgundy">Dukes of Burgundy</a> used the term <i>les pays de par deçà</i> ("the lands over here") for the Low Countries.<sup id="cite_ref-BlockmansPrevenier2010_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BlockmansPrevenier2010-43">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Under <a href="/wiki/Habsburg_Netherlands" title="Habsburg Netherlands">Habsburg rule</a>, <i>Les pays de par deçà</i> developed in <i>pays d'embas</i> ("lands down-here").<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> This was translated as <i>Neder-landen</i> in contemporary Dutch official documents.<sup id="cite_ref-Lem_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lem-45">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> From a regional point of view, <i>Niderlant</i> was also the area between the <a href="/wiki/Meuse_(river)" class="mw-redirect" title="Meuse (river)">Meuse</a> and the lower <a href="/wiki/Rhine" title="Rhine">Rhine</a> in the late Middle Ages. From the mid-sixteenth century, the "Low Countries" and the "Netherlands" lost their original <a href="/wiki/Deixis" title="Deixis">deictic meaning</a>.
</p><p>In most <a href="/wiki/Romance_languages" title="Romance languages">Romance languages</a>, the term "Low Countries" is officially used as the name for the Netherlands.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Holland_and_Dutch">Holland and Dutch</span></h3>
<p>The Netherlands is <a href="/wiki/Terminology_of_the_Low_Countries#Holland_(pars_pro_toto)" title="Terminology of the Low Countries">informally</a> referred to as <a href="/wiki/Holland" title="Holland">Holland</a> in various languages, including Dutch<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> and English. In other languages, Holland is the formal name for the Netherlands. Holland can also refer to a region within the Netherlands that consists of <a href="/wiki/North_Holland" title="North Holland">North</a> and <a href="/wiki/South_Holland" title="South Holland">South Holland</a>. Formerly these were a single province, and earlier the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Holland" title="County of Holland">County of Holland</a>, a remnant of the dissolved <a href="/wiki/Frisian_Kingdom" title="Frisian Kingdom">Frisian Kingdom</a> that also included parts of present-day <a href="/wiki/Utrecht_(province)" title="Utrecht (province)">Utrecht</a>. Following the decline of the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Brabant" title="Duchy of Brabant">Duchy of Brabant</a> and the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Flanders" title="County of Flanders">County of Flanders</a>, Holland became the most economically and politically important county in the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a> region. The emphasis on Holland during the formation of the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Eighty_Years%27_War" title="Eighty Years&#39; War">Eighty Years' War</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Dutch_Wars" title="Anglo-Dutch Wars">Anglo-Dutch Wars</a> in the 16th, 17th, and 18th centuries, made Holland a <i><a href="/wiki/Pars_pro_toto" title="Pars pro toto">pars pro toto</a></i> for the entire country.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><i>Dutch</i> is used as the <a href="/wiki/Adjective" title="Adjective">adjective</a> for the Netherlands, as in well as the <a href="/wiki/Demonym" title="Demonym">demonym</a>. The origins of the word go back to Proto-Germanic <i>*þiudiskaz</i>, <a href="/wiki/Onomastic_Latinisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Onomastic Latinisation">Latinised</a> into <a href="/wiki/Theodiscus" title="Theodiscus">Theodiscus</a>, meaning "popular" or "of the people"; akin to Old Dutch <i>Dietsch</i>, Old High German <i>duitsch</i>, and Old English <i>þeodisc</i>, all meaning "(of) the common (Germanic) people". At first, the English language used Dutch to refer to any or all speakers of <a href="/wiki/West_Germanic_languages" title="West Germanic languages">West Germanic languages</a>. Gradually its meaning shifted to the West Germanic people they had the most contact with, because of their geographical proximity and rivalry in trade and overseas territories.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Netherlands" title="History of the Netherlands">History of the Netherlands</a></div>
<h3><span id="Prehistory_.28before_800_BC.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prehistory_(before_800_BC)">Prehistory (before 800 BC)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Paleolithic_Europe" title="Paleolithic Europe">Paleolithic Europe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Neolithic_Europe" title="Neolithic Europe">Neolithic Europe</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age_Europe" title="Bronze Age Europe">Bronze Age Europe</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg/170px-Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="303" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg/255px-Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg/340px-Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg 2x" data-file-width="620" data-file-height="1104" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mannetje_van_Willemstad.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Oak figurine found in <a href="/wiki/Willemstad,_North_Brabant" title="Willemstad, North Brabant">Willemstad</a> (4500 BC)</div></div></div>
<p>The prehistory of the area that is now the Netherlands was largely shaped by the sea and the rivers that constantly shifted the low-lying geography. The oldest human (<a href="/wiki/Neanderthal" title="Neanderthal">Neanderthal</a>) traces, believed to be about 250,000 years old, were found in higher soils near <a href="/wiki/Maastricht" title="Maastricht">Maastricht</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> At the end of the <a href="/wiki/Weichselian_glaciation" title="Weichselian glaciation">Ice Age</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Nomadic" class="mw-redirect" title="Nomadic">nomadic</a> late <a href="/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic" title="Upper Paleolithic">Upper Palaeolithic</a> <a href="/wiki/Hamburg_culture" title="Hamburg culture">Hamburg culture</a> (13,000–10,000 BC) hunted <a href="/wiki/Reindeer" title="Reindeer">reindeer</a> in the area, using spears. The later <a href="/wiki/Ahrensburg_culture" title="Ahrensburg culture">Ahrensburg culture</a> (11,200–9,500 BC) used <a href="/wiki/Bow_and_arrow" title="Bow and arrow">bow and arrow</a>. From <a href="/wiki/Mesolithic_Europe" class="mw-redirect" title="Mesolithic Europe">Mesolithic</a> <a href="/wiki/Maglemosian_culture" title="Maglemosian culture">Maglemosian-like tribes</a> (c. 8000 BC), the world's <a href="/wiki/Pesse_canoe" title="Pesse canoe">oldest canoe</a> was found in <a href="/wiki/Drenthe" title="Drenthe">Drenthe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Indigenous late Mesolithic <a href="/wiki/Hunter-gatherer" title="Hunter-gatherer">hunter-gatherers</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Swifterbant_culture" title="Swifterbant culture">Swifterbant culture</a> (c. 5600 BC), related to the southern Scandinavian <a href="/wiki/Erteb%C3%B8lle_culture" title="Ertebølle culture">Ertebølle culture</a>, were strongly linked to rivers and open water.<sup id="cite_ref-Kooijmans1998_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kooijmans1998-51">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Between 4800 and 4500 BC, the Swifterbant people started to adopt from the neighbouring <a href="/wiki/Linear_Pottery_culture" title="Linear Pottery culture">Linear Pottery culture</a> the practice of <a href="/wiki/Animal_husbandry" title="Animal husbandry">animal husbandry</a>, and between 4300 and 4000 BC the practice of <a href="/wiki/Agriculture" title="Agriculture">agriculture</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Funnelbeaker_culture" title="Funnelbeaker culture">Funnelbeaker culture</a> (4300–2800 BC), related to the Swifterbant culture, erected the <a href="/wiki/Dolmens" class="mw-redirect" title="Dolmens">dolmens</a>, large stone grave monuments found in <a href="/wiki/Drenthe" title="Drenthe">Drenthe</a>. There was a quick and smooth transition from the Funnelbeaker farming culture to the pan-European <a href="/wiki/Corded_Ware" class="mw-redirect" title="Corded Ware">Corded Ware</a> <a href="/wiki/Pastoralism" title="Pastoralism">pastoralist</a> culture (c. 2950 BC). In the southwest, the <a href="/wiki/Seine-Oise-Marne_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="Seine-Oise-Marne culture">Seine-Oise-Marne culture</a> — related to the <a href="/wiki/Vlaardingen_culture" title="Vlaardingen culture">Vlaardingen culture</a> (c. 2600 BC), an apparently more primitive culture of hunter-gatherers — survived well into the <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">Neolithic</a> period, until it too was succeeded by the Corded Ware culture.
</p>

.trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:342px;max-width:342px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:137px;max-width:137px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:163px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:5500vc_ex_leg.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/5500vc_ex_leg.jpg/135px-5500vc_ex_leg.jpg" decoding="async" width="135" height="164" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/5500vc_ex_leg.jpg/203px-5500vc_ex_leg.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/5500vc_ex_leg.jpg/270px-5500vc_ex_leg.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="607" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center">The Netherlands in 5500 BC</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:201px;max-width:201px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:163px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:BronzAgeElp.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/BronzAgeElp.png/199px-BronzAgeElp.png" decoding="async" width="199" height="163" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/BronzAgeElp.png/299px-BronzAgeElp.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/BronzAgeElp.png/398px-BronzAgeElp.png 2x" data-file-width="606" data-file-height="497" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center">Bronze Age cultures in the Netherlands</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>The subsequent <a href="/wiki/Beaker_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="Beaker culture">Bell Beaker culture</a> (2700–2100 BC)<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> introduced metalwork in copper, gold and later bronze and opened international trade routes not seen before, reflected in <a href="/wiki/Copper_Age_Europe" class="mw-redirect" title="Copper Age Europe">copper artifacts</a>. Finds of rare bronze objects suggest that Drenthe was a trading centre in the Bronze Age (2000–800 BC). The Bell Beaker culture developed locally into the Barbed-Wire Beaker culture (2100–1800 BC) and later the <a href="/wiki/Elp_culture" title="Elp culture">Elp culture</a> (1800–800 BC),<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> a Middle Bronze Age archaeological culture with <a href="/wiki/Earthenware" title="Earthenware">earthenware</a> low-quality pottery as a marker. The initial phase of the Elp culture was characterised by <a href="/wiki/Tumulus" title="Tumulus">tumuli</a> (1800–1200 BC). The subsequent phase was that of cremating the dead and placing their ashes in urns which were then buried in fields, following the customs of the <a href="/wiki/Urnfield_culture" title="Urnfield culture">Urnfield culture</a> (1200–800 BC). The southern region became dominated by the related <a href="/wiki/Hilversum_culture" title="Hilversum culture">Hilversum culture</a> (1800–800 BC), with apparently cultural ties with <a href="/wiki/British_Isles" title="British Isles">Britain</a> of the previous Barbed-Wire Beaker culture.
</p>
<h3><span id="Celts.2C_Germanic_tribes_and_Romans_.28800_BC.E2.80.93410_AD.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Celts,_Germanic_tribes_and_Romans_(800_BC–410_AD)">Celts, Germanic tribes and Romans (800 BC–410 AD)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Iron_Age_Europe" title="Iron Age Europe">Iron Age Europe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Celts" title="Celts">Celts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germanic_peoples" title="Germanic peoples">Germanic peoples</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Netherlands_in_the_Roman_era" title="Netherlands in the Roman era">Netherlands in the Roman era</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg/220px-Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg/330px-Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg/440px-Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="933" data-file-height="690" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Celtic_expansion_in_Europe.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#FFFF43; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Historical_linguistics" title="Historical linguistics">Diachronic</a> distribution of <a href="/wiki/Celts" title="Celts">Celts</a> from 500 BC</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"/><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#97FFB6; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;Expansion into the southern <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a> by 270 BC</div></div></div></div>
<p>From 800 BC onwards, the <a href="/wiki/Iron_Age_Europe" title="Iron Age Europe">Iron Age</a> Celtic <a href="/wiki/Hallstatt_culture" title="Hallstatt culture">Hallstatt culture</a> became influential, replacing the <a href="/wiki/Hilversum_culture" title="Hilversum culture">Hilversum culture</a>. Iron ore brought a measure of prosperity and was available throughout the country, including <a href="/wiki/Bog_iron" title="Bog iron">bog iron</a>. <a href="/wiki/Blacksmith" title="Blacksmith">Smiths</a> travelled from settlement to settlement with bronze and iron, fabricating tools on demand. The <a href="/wiki/Vorstengraf_(Oss)" title="Vorstengraf (Oss)">King's grave of Oss</a> (700 BC) was found in a burial mound, the largest of its kind in Western Europe and containing an iron sword with an inlay of gold and coral.
</p><p>The deteriorating climate in Scandinavia around 850 BC further deteriorated around 650 BC and might have triggered the migration of <a href="/wiki/Germanic_peoples" title="Germanic peoples">Germanic</a> tribes from the North. By the time this migration was complete, around 250 BC, a few general cultural and linguistic groups had emerged.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Verhaal_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verhaal-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/North_Sea_Germanic" title="North Sea Germanic">North Sea Germanic</a> <a href="/wiki/Ingaevones" title="Ingaevones">Ingaevones</a> inhabited the northern part of the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a>. They would later develop into the <a href="/wiki/Frisii" title="Frisii">Frisii</a> and the early <a href="/wiki/Saxons" title="Saxons">Saxons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Verhaal_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verhaal-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> A second grouping, the <a href="/wiki/Weser-Rhine_Germanic" title="Weser-Rhine Germanic">Weser-Rhine Germanic</a> (or <a href="/wiki/Istvaeones" title="Istvaeones">Istvaeones</a>), extended along the middle Rhine and <a href="/wiki/Weser" title="Weser">Weser</a> and inhabited the Low Countries south of the great rivers. This group consisted of tribes that would eventually develop into the <a href="/wiki/Salian_Franks" title="Salian Franks">Salian Franks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Verhaal_56-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verhaal-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Also the <a href="/wiki/Celts" title="Celts">Celtic</a> <a href="/wiki/La_T%C3%A8ne_culture" title="La Tène culture">La Tène culture</a> (c. 450 BC up to the Roman conquest) had expanded over a wide range, including the southern area of the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a>. Some scholars have speculated that even a third ethnic identity and language, neither Germanic nor Celtic, survived in the Netherlands until the Roman period, the Iron Age <a href="/wiki/Nordwestblock" title="Nordwestblock">Nordwestblock</a> culture,<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Lendering_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lendering-58">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> that eventually was absorbed by the <a href="/wiki/Celts" title="Celts">Celts</a> to the south and the Germanic peoples from the east.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Germania_70.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Germania_70.svg/220px-Germania_70.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Germania_70.svg/330px-Germania_70.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Germania_70.svg/440px-Germania_70.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="972" data-file-height="780" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Germania_70.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Rhine frontier around 70 AD</div></div></div>
<p>The first author to describe the coast of <a href="/wiki/Holland" title="Holland">Holland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Flanders" title="Flanders">Flanders</a> was the <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greece" title="Ancient Greece">Greek</a> <a href="/wiki/Geographer" title="Geographer">geographer</a> <a href="/wiki/Pytheas" title="Pytheas">Pytheas</a>, who noted in c. 325 BC that in these regions, "more people died in the struggle against water than in the struggle against men."<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Gallic_Wars" title="Gallic Wars">Gallic Wars</a>, the area south and west of the <a href="/wiki/Rhine" title="Rhine">Rhine</a> was conquered by <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman forces</a> under <a href="/wiki/Julius_Caesar" title="Julius Caesar">Julius Caesar</a> from 57 BC to 53 BC.<sup id="cite_ref-Lendering_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lendering-58">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Caesar describes two main Celtic tribes living in what is now the southern Netherlands: the <a href="/wiki/Menapii" title="Menapii">Menapii</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Eburones" title="Eburones">Eburones</a>. The Rhine became fixed as Rome's northern frontier around 12 AD. Notable towns would arise along the <a href="/wiki/Limes_Germanicus" title="Limes Germanicus">Limes Germanicus</a>: <a href="/wiki/Nijmegen" title="Nijmegen">Nijmegen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Voorburg" title="Voorburg">Voorburg</a>. In the first part of <a href="/wiki/Gallia_Belgica" title="Gallia Belgica">Gallia Belgica</a>, the area south of the Limes became part of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_province" title="Roman province">Roman province</a> of <a href="/wiki/Germania_Inferior" title="Germania Inferior">Germania Inferior</a>. The area to the north of the Rhine, inhabited by the Frisii, remained outside Roman rule (but not its presence and control), while the Germanic border tribes of the <a href="/wiki/Batavi_(Germanic_tribe)" title="Batavi (Germanic tribe)">Batavi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cananefates" title="Cananefates">Cananefates</a> served in the <a href="/wiki/Ala_(Roman_allied_military_unit)" title="Ala (Roman allied military unit)">Roman cavalry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The Batavi rose against the Romans in the <a href="/wiki/Revolt_of_the_Batavi" title="Revolt of the Batavi">Batavian rebellion</a> of 69 AD but were eventually defeated. The Batavi later merged with other tribes into the confederation of the Salian Franks, whose identity emerged in the first half of the third century.<sup id="cite_ref-Previté-Orton_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Previté-Orton-61">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Salian Franks appear in Roman texts as both allies and enemies. They were forced by the confederation of the Saxons from the east to move over the Rhine into Roman territory in the fourth century. From their new base in <a href="/wiki/West_Flanders" title="West Flanders">West Flanders</a> and the Southwest Netherlands, they were raiding the <a href="/wiki/English_Channel" title="English Channel">English Channel</a>. Roman forces pacified the region but did not expel the Franks, who continued to be feared at least until the time of <a href="/wiki/Julian_(emperor)" title="Julian (emperor)">Julian the Apostate</a> (358) when Salian Franks were allowed to settle as <i><a href="/wiki/Foederati" title="Foederati">foederati</a></i> in <a href="/wiki/Texandria" title="Texandria">Texandria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Previté-Orton_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Previté-Orton-61">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> It has been postulated that after deteriorating climate conditions and the Romans' withdrawal, the <a href="/wiki/Frisii" title="Frisii">Frisii</a> disappeared as <i><a href="/wiki/Laeti" title="Laeti">laeti</a></i> in c. 296, leaving the coastal lands largely unpopulated for the next two centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> However, recent excavations in <a href="/wiki/Kennemerland" title="Kennemerland">Kennemerland</a> show a clear indication of permanent habitation.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Early_Middle_Ages_.28411.E2.80.931000.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_Middle_Ages_(411–1000)">Early Middle Ages (411–1000)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Frankish_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Frankish Kingdom">Frankish Kingdom</a> and <a href="/wiki/Frisian_Kingdom" title="Frisian Kingdom">Frisian Kingdom</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Frisia_716-la.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Frisia_716-la.svg/170px-Frisia_716-la.svg.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="176" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Frisia_716-la.svg/255px-Frisia_716-la.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Frisia_716-la.svg/340px-Frisia_716-la.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="583" data-file-height="602" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Frisia_716-la.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Franks, Frisians and Saxons (710s AD) with <a href="/wiki/Traiectum_(Utrecht)" title="Traiectum (Utrecht)">Traiectum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dorestad" title="Dorestad">Dorestad</a> in the middle</div></div></div>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman</a> government in the area collapsed, the Franks expanded their territories into numerous kingdoms. By the 490s, <a href="/wiki/Clovis_I" title="Clovis I">Clovis I</a> had conquered and united all these territories in the southern Netherlands in one <a href="/wiki/Frankish_kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Frankish kingdom">Frankish kingdom</a>, and from there continued his conquests into <a href="/wiki/Gaul" title="Gaul">Gaul</a>. During this expansion, Franks migrating to the south (modern territory of France and Walloon part of Belgium) eventually adopted the <a href="/wiki/Vulgar_Latin" title="Vulgar Latin">Vulgar Latin</a> of the local population.<sup id="cite_ref-Verhaal_56-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verhaal-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> A widening cultural divide grew with the Franks remaining in their original homeland in the north (i.e. the southern Netherlands and Flanders), who kept on speaking <a href="/wiki/Old_Frankish" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Frankish">Old Frankish</a>, which by the ninth century had evolved into <a href="/wiki/Old_Low_Franconian" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Low Franconian">Old Low Franconian</a> or <a href="/wiki/Old_Dutch" title="Old Dutch">Old Dutch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Verhaal_56-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verhaal-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> A Dutch-French language boundary hence came into existence.<sup id="cite_ref-Verhaal_56-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Verhaal-56">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Franks_expansion.gif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Franks_expansion.gif/220px-Franks_expansion.gif" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Franks_expansion.gif/330px-Franks_expansion.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Franks_expansion.gif/440px-Franks_expansion.gif 2x" data-file-width="790" data-file-height="505" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Franks_expansion.gif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Frankish expansion (481 to 870 AD)</div></div></div>
<p>To the north of the Franks, climatic conditions improved, and during the <a href="/wiki/Migration_Period" title="Migration Period">Migration Period</a> <a href="/wiki/Saxons" title="Saxons">Saxons</a>, the closely related <a href="/wiki/Angles" title="Angles">Angles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jutes" title="Jutes">Jutes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Frisii" title="Frisii">Frisii</a> settled the coastal land.<sup id="cite_ref-Ethnic_Constructs_in_Antiquity_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ethnic_Constructs_in_Antiquity-66">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Many moved on to <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a> and came to be known as <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxons" title="Anglo-Saxons">Anglo-Saxons</a>, but those who stayed would be referred to as <a href="/wiki/Frisians" title="Frisians">Frisians</a> and their language as <a href="/wiki/Frisian_languages" title="Frisian languages">Frisian</a>, named after the land that was once inhabited by Frisii.<sup id="cite_ref-Ethnic_Constructs_in_Antiquity_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ethnic_Constructs_in_Antiquity-66">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Frisian was spoken along the entire southern North Sea coast, and it is still the language most closely related to English among the living languages of continental Europe. By the seventh century, a <a href="/wiki/Frisian_Kingdom" title="Frisian Kingdom">Frisian Kingdom</a> (650–734) under King <a href="/wiki/Aldegisel" class="mw-redirect" title="Aldegisel">Aldegisel</a> and King <a href="/wiki/Redbad,_King_of_the_Frisians" title="Redbad, King of the Frisians">Redbad</a> emerged with <a href="/wiki/Traiectum_(Utrecht)" title="Traiectum (Utrecht)">Traiectum</a> (<a href="/wiki/Utrecht_(city)" class="mw-redirect" title="Utrecht (city)">Utrecht</a>) as its centre of power,<sup id="cite_ref-Ethnic_Constructs_in_Antiquity_66-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ethnic_Constructs_in_Antiquity-66">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> while <a href="/wiki/Dorestad" title="Dorestad">Dorestad</a> was a flourishing trading place.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Atlas_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Atlas-69">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Between 600 and around 719 the cities were often fought over between the Frisians and the Franks. In 734, at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Boarn" title="Battle of the Boarn">Battle of the Boarn</a>, the Frisians were defeated after a <a href="/wiki/Frisian%E2%80%93Frankish_wars" title="Frisian–Frankish wars">series of wars</a>. With the approval of the Franks, the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_mission" title="Anglo-Saxon mission">Anglo-Saxon</a> missionary <a href="/wiki/Willibrord" title="Willibrord">Willibrord</a> converted the Frisian people to <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>. He established the <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Utrecht_(695%E2%80%931580)" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Utrecht (695–1580)">Archdiocese of Utrecht</a> and became the bishop of the Frisians. However, his successor <a href="/wiki/Boniface" class="mw-redirect" title="Boniface">Boniface</a> was murdered by the Frisians in <a href="/wiki/Dokkum" title="Dokkum">Dokkum</a>, in 754.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg/170px-Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="239" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg/255px-Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg/340px-Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="843" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Rorik_by_H._W._Koekkoek.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Rorik_of_Dorestad" title="Rorik of Dorestad">Rorik of Dorestad</a>, <a href="/wiki/Viking" class="mw-redirect" title="Viking">Viking</a> ruler of <a href="/wiki/Friesland" title="Friesland">Friesland</a> (romantic 1912 depiction)</div></div></div>
<p>The Frankish <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Carolingian empire">Carolingian empire</a> modelled itself on the Roman Empire and controlled much of Western Europe. However, in 843, it was divided into three parts—<a href="/wiki/East_Francia" title="East Francia">East</a>, <a href="/wiki/Middle_Francia" title="Middle Francia">Middle</a>, and <a href="/wiki/West_Francia" title="West Francia">West Francia</a>. Most of present-day Netherlands became part of <a href="/wiki/Middle_Francia" title="Middle Francia">Middle Francia</a>, which was a weak kingdom and subject to numerous partitions and annexation attempts by its stronger neighbours. It comprised territories from <a href="/wiki/Frisia" title="Frisia">Frisia</a> in the north to the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy_(medieval)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Italy (medieval)">Kingdom of Italy</a> in the south. Around 850, <a href="/wiki/Lothair_I" title="Lothair I">Lothair I</a> of Middle Francia acknowledged the Viking <a href="/wiki/Rorik_of_Dorestad" title="Rorik of Dorestad">Rorik of Dorestad</a> as ruler of most of Frisia.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> When the kingdom of Middle Francia was partitioned in 855, the lands north of the <a href="/wiki/Alps" title="Alps">Alps</a> passed to <a href="/wiki/Lothair_II_of_Lotharingia" class="mw-redirect" title="Lothair II of Lotharingia">Lothair II</a> and subsequently were named <a href="/wiki/Lotharingia" title="Lotharingia">Lotharingia</a>. After he died in 869, Lotharingia was partitioned, into <a href="/wiki/Upper_Lotharingia" class="mw-redirect" title="Upper Lotharingia">Upper</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lower_Lotharingia" title="Lower Lotharingia">Lower Lotharingia</a>, the latter part comprising the Low Countries that technically became part of <a href="/wiki/East_Francia" title="East Francia">East Francia</a> in 870, although it was effectively under the control of Vikings, who raided the largely defenceless <a href="/wiki/Frisians" title="Frisians">Frisian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Frankish</a> towns lying on the Frisian coast and along the rivers.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Around 879, another Viking expedition led by <a href="/wiki/Godfrid,_Duke_of_Frisia" title="Godfrid, Duke of Frisia">Godfrid, Duke of Frisia</a>, raided the Frisian lands. The Viking raids made the sway of French and German lords in the area weak. Resistance to the Vikings, if any, came from local nobles, who gained in stature as a result, and that laid the basis for the disintegration of Lower Lotharingia into semi-independent states. One of these local nobles was <a href="/wiki/Gerolf_of_Holland" title="Gerolf of Holland">Gerolf of Holland</a>, who assumed lordship in Frisia after he helped to assassinate Godfrid, and Viking rule came to an end.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="High_Middle_Ages_.281000.E2.80.931384.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="High_Middle_Ages_(1000–1384)">High Middle Ages (1000–1384)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_urban_centres_in_the_Low_Countries" title="History of urban centres in the Low Countries">History of urban centres in the Low Countries</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg/220px-Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="197" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg/330px-Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg/440px-Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1115" data-file-height="1000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Graftombe_Ridder_Arnold_van_der_Sluijs.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A medieval tomb of the Brabantian knight Arnold van der Sluijs</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a> (the successor state of East Francia and then Lotharingia) ruled much of the Low Countries in the 10th and 11th century but was not able to maintain political unity. Powerful local nobles turned their cities, counties and duchies into private kingdoms that felt little sense of obligation to the emperor.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/wiki/County_of_Holland" title="County of Holland">Holland</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_of_Hainaut" title="County of Hainaut">Hainaut</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_of_Flanders" title="County of Flanders">Flanders</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Gelre" class="mw-redirect" title="Duchy of Gelre">Gelre</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Brabant" title="Duchy of Brabant">Brabant</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bishopric_of_Utrecht" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishopric of Utrecht">Utrecht</a> were in a state of almost continual war or paradoxically formed personal unions. The language and culture of most of the people who lived in the County of Holland were originally <a href="/wiki/Frisia" title="Frisia">Frisian</a>. As Frankish settlement progressed from Flanders and Brabant, the area quickly became <a href="/wiki/Old_Low_Franconian" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Low Franconian">Old Low Franconian</a> (or <a href="/wiki/Old_Dutch" title="Old Dutch">Old Dutch</a>). The rest of <a href="/wiki/Frisia" title="Frisia">Frisia</a> in the north (now <a href="/wiki/Friesland" title="Friesland">Friesland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Groningen_(province)" title="Groningen (province)">Groningen</a>) continued to maintain its independence and had its own institutions (collectively called the "<a href="/wiki/Frisian_freedom" title="Frisian freedom">Frisian freedom</a>"), which resented the imposition of the feudal system.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Around 1000 AD, due to several agricultural developments, the economy started to develop at a fast pace, and the higher productivity allowed workers to farm more land or become tradesmen. Towns grew around <a href="/wiki/Monasteries" class="mw-redirect" title="Monasteries">monasteries</a> and <a href="/wiki/Castles" class="mw-redirect" title="Castles">castles</a>, and a mercantile middle class began to develop in these urban areas, especially in Flanders and later also Brabant. Wealthy cities started to buy certain <a href="/wiki/City_rights_in_the_Low_Countries" title="City rights in the Low Countries">privileges</a> for themselves from the <a href="/wiki/Monarch" title="Monarch">sovereign</a>. In practice, this meant that <a href="/wiki/Bruges" title="Bruges">Bruges</a> and <a href="/wiki/Antwerp" title="Antwerp">Antwerp</a> became quasi-independent republics in their own right and would later develop into some of the most important cities and ports in Europe.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Around 1100 AD, farmers from <a href="/wiki/County_of_Flanders" title="County of Flanders">Flanders</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bishopric_of_Utrecht" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishopric of Utrecht">Utrecht</a> began draining and cultivating uninhabited swampy land in the western Netherlands, making the emergence of the County of Holland as the centre of power possible. The title of <a href="/wiki/Count_of_Holland" title="Count of Holland">Count of Holland</a> was fought over in the <a href="/wiki/Hook_and_Cod_Wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Hook and Cod Wars">Hook and Cod Wars</a> (<a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a>: <i lang="nl">Hoekse en Kabeljauwse twisten</i>) between 1350 and 1490. The Cod faction consisted of the more progressive cities, while the Hook faction consisted of the conservative noblemen. These noblemen invited Duke <a href="/wiki/Philip_the_Good" title="Philip the Good">Philip the Good</a> of Burgundy — who was also Count of Flanders — to conquer Holland.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Burgundian.2C_Habsburg_and_Spanish_Habsburg_Netherlands_.281384.E2.80.931581.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Burgundian,_Habsburg_and_Spanish_Habsburg_Netherlands_(1384–1581)">Burgundian, Habsburg and Spanish Habsburg Netherlands (1384–1581)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Burgundian_Netherlands" title="Burgundian Netherlands">Burgundian Netherlands</a>, <a href="/wiki/Habsburg_Netherlands" title="Habsburg Netherlands">Habsburg Netherlands</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Netherlands" title="Spanish Netherlands">Spanish Netherlands</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tleft"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:242px;max-width:242px"><div class="trow"><div class="theader">Spanish Netherlands</div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:240px;max-width:240px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:175px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Van_Soest,_Four_Days_Battle.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Van_Soest%2C_Four_Days_Battle.jpg/238px-Van_Soest%2C_Four_Days_Battle.jpg" decoding="async" width="238" height="175" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Van_Soest%2C_Four_Days_Battle.jpg/357px-Van_Soest%2C_Four_Days_Battle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Van_Soest%2C_Four_Days_Battle.jpg/476px-Van_Soest%2C_Four_Days_Battle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="516" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center"><i>The <a href="/wiki/Four_Days%27_Battle" title="Four Days&#39; Battle">Four Days' Battle</a>, 1–4 June 1666</i> (<a href="/wiki/Second_Anglo-Dutch_War" title="Second Anglo-Dutch War">Second Anglo-Dutch War</a>) by <a href="/wiki/Pieter_Cornelisz_van_Soest" title="Pieter Cornelisz van Soest">Pieter Cornelisz van Soest</a></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:123px;max-width:123px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:155px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_(1350)-NL.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_%281350%29-NL.svg/121px-Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_%281350%29-NL.svg.png" decoding="async" width="121" height="155" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_%281350%29-NL.svg/182px-Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_%281350%29-NL.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_%281350%29-NL.svg/242px-Political_map_of_the_Low_Countries_%281350%29-NL.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="2818" data-file-height="3608" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center">The Low Countries in the late 14th century</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:115px;max-width:115px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:155px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:William_I,_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/William_I%2C_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg/113px-William_I%2C_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg" decoding="async" width="113" height="156" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/William_I%2C_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg/170px-William_I%2C_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/William_I%2C_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg/226px-William_I%2C_Prince_of_Orange_by_Adriaen_Thomasz._Key_Rijksmuseum_Amsterdam_SK-A-3148.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4876" data-file-height="6728" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center"><a href="/wiki/William_the_Silent" title="William the Silent">William I</a>, Prince of Orange, leader of the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Revolt" class="mw-redirect" title="Dutch Revolt">Dutch Revolt</a>, by <a href="/wiki/Adriaen_Thomasz._Key" class="mw-redirect" title="Adriaen Thomasz. Key">Adriaen Thomasz. Key</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Most of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Imperial</a> and <a href="/wiki/France_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="France in the Middle Ages">French</a> fiefs in what is now the Netherlands and Belgium were united in a <a href="/wiki/Personal_union" title="Personal union">personal union</a> by Philip the Good, Duke of <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Burgundy" title="Duchy of Burgundy">Burgundy</a>, in 1433. The <a href="/wiki/House_of_Valois-Burgundy" title="House of Valois-Burgundy">House of Valois-Burgundy</a> and their <a href="/wiki/House_of_Habsburg" title="House of Habsburg">Habsburg</a> heirs would rule the Low Countries in the period from 1384 to 1581. Before the Burgundian union, the Dutch identified themselves by the town they lived in or their local duchy or county. The Burgundian period is when the road to nationhood began. The new rulers defended Dutch trading interests, which then developed rapidly. The fleets of the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Holland" title="County of Holland">County of Holland</a> defeated the fleets of the <a href="/wiki/Hanseatic_League" title="Hanseatic League">Hanseatic League</a> several times. <a href="/wiki/Amsterdam" title="Amsterdam">Amsterdam</a> grew and in the 15th century became the primary trading port in Europe for grain from the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_region" title="Baltic region">Baltic region</a>. Amsterdam distributed grain to the major cities of Belgium, Northern France and England. This trade was vital because Holland could no longer produce enough grain to feed itself. Land drainage had caused the <a href="/wiki/Peat" title="Peat">peat</a> of the former <a href="/wiki/Wetland" title="Wetland">wetlands</a> to reduce to a level that was too low for drainage to be maintained.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Under Habsburg <a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles V</a>, ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a> and King of <a href="/wiki/Habsburg_Spain" title="Habsburg Spain">Spain</a>, all fiefs in the current Netherlands region were united into the <a href="/wiki/Seventeen_Provinces" title="Seventeen Provinces">Seventeen Provinces</a>, which also included most of present-day Belgium, Luxembourg, and some adjacent land in what is now France and Germany. In 1568, under Phillip II, the <a href="/wiki/Eighty_Years%27_War" title="Eighty Years&#39; War">Eighty Years' War</a> between the Provinces and their <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish</a> ruler began. The level of ferocity exhibited by both sides can be gleaned from a Dutch chronicler's report:<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p> On more than one occasion men were seen hanging their own brothers, who had been taken prisoners in the enemy's ranks... A Spaniard had ceased to be human in their eyes. On one occasion, a surgeon at Veer cut the heart from a Spanish prisoner, nailed it on a vessel's prow, and invited the townsmen to come and fasten their teeth in it, which many did with savage satisfaction. </p></blockquote>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Fernando_%C3%81lvarez_de_Toledo,_3rd_Duke_of_Alba" title="Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, 3rd Duke of Alba">Duke of Alba</a> ruthlessly attempted to suppress the Protestant movement in the Netherlands. Netherlanders were "burned, strangled, beheaded, or buried alive" by his "<a href="/wiki/Council_of_Troubles" title="Council of Troubles">Blood Council</a>" and his Spanish soldiers. Severed heads and decapitated corpses were displayed along streets and roads to terrorise the population into submission. Alba boasted of having executed 18,600,<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> but this figure does not include those who perished by war and famine.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The first great siege was Alba's effort to capture <a href="/wiki/Haarlem" title="Haarlem">Haarlem</a> and thereby cut Holland in half. It dragged on from December 1572 to the next summer, when Haarlemers finally surrendered on 13 July upon the promise that the city would be spared from being sacked. It was a stipulation <a href="/wiki/Fadrique_%C3%81lvarez_de_Toledo,_4th_Duke_of_Alba" title="Fadrique Álvarez de Toledo, 4th Duke of Alba">Don Fadrique</a> was unable to honour, when his soldiers mutinied, angered over pay owed and the miserable conditions they endured during the long, cold months of the campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> On 4 November 1576, Spanish <a href="/wiki/Tercio" title="Tercio">tercios</a> seized <a href="/wiki/Antwerp" title="Antwerp">Antwerp</a> and subjected it to the worst pillage in the Netherlands' history. The citizens resisted but were overcome; seven thousand of them were killed; a thousand buildings were torched; men, women, and children were slaughtered by soldiers, who invoked the name of Spain's patron saint, <i>¡Santiago! ¡España! ¡A sangre, a carne, a fuego, a sacco!</i> (Saint James! Spain! To blood, to the flesh, to fire, to sack!)<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg/220px-Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="137" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg/330px-Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg/440px-Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1654" data-file-height="1029" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Habsburg_Map_1547.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of the <a href="/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy" title="Habsburg monarchy">Habsburg</a> dominions. From 1556 the dynasty's lands in the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a> were retained by the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Habsburgs" class="mw-redirect" title="Spanish Habsburgs">Spanish Habsburgs</a>.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Beleg_van_Maastricht_(1579),_Johann_Jakob_Wick_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Beleg_van_Maastricht_%281579%29%2C_Johann_Jakob_Wick_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Beleg_van_Maastricht_%281579%29%2C_Johann_Jakob_Wick_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Beleg_van_Maastricht_%281579%29%2C_Johann_Jakob_Wick_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Beleg_van_Maastricht_%281579%29%2C_Johann_Jakob_Wick_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Beleg_van_Maastricht_%281579%29%2C_Johann_Jakob_Wick_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Beleg_van_Maastricht_%281579%29%2C_Johann_Jakob_Wick_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="565" data-file-height="382" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Beleg_van_Maastricht_(1579),_Johann_Jakob_Wick_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Fury" title="Spanish Fury">Spanish Fury</a> at <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Maastricht_(1579)" title="Siege of Maastricht (1579)">Maastricht</a>, 1579</div></div></div>
<p>Following the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Antwerp" title="Sack of Antwerp">sack of Antwerp</a>, delegates from Catholic Brabant, Protestant Holland and Zeeland agreed, at Ghent, to join Utrecht and William the Silent in driving out all Spanish troops and forming a new government for the Netherlands. <a href="/wiki/Don_Juan_of_Austria" class="mw-redirect" title="Don Juan of Austria">Don Juan of Austria</a>, the new Spanish governor, was forced to concede initially, but within months returned to active hostilities. As the fighting restarted, the Dutch began to look for help from the Protestant <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_I_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth I of England">Elizabeth I of England</a>, but she initially stood by her commitments to the Spanish in the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Bristol" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Bristol">Treaty of Bristol</a> of 1574.  The result was that when the next large-scale battle did occur at <a href="/wiki/Gembloux" title="Gembloux">Gembloux</a> in 1578, the Spanish forces easily won the day, killing at least 10,000 rebels, with the Spanish suffering few losses.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement" title="Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute"><span title="The material near this tag is possibly inaccurate or nonfactual. (May 2022)">dubious</span></a>&#32;<span class="metadata"> &#8211; <a href="/wiki/Talk:Netherlands#Dubious" title="Talk:Netherlands">discuss</a></span></i>&#93;</sup> In light of the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Gembloux_(1578)" title="Battle of Gembloux (1578)">defeat at Gembloux</a>, the southern states of the Seventeen Provinces (today in northern France and Belgium) distanced themselves from the rebels in the north with the 1579 <a href="/wiki/Union_of_Arras" title="Union of Arras">Union of Arras</a>, which expressed their loyalty to <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain" title="Philip II of Spain">Philip II of Spain</a>. Opposing them, the northern half of the Seventeen Provinces forged the <a href="/wiki/Union_of_Utrecht" title="Union of Utrecht">Union of Utrecht</a> (also of 1579) in which they committed to support each other in their defence against the Spanish army.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The Union of Utrecht is seen as the foundation of the modern Netherlands.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Spanish troops sacked <a href="/wiki/Maastricht" title="Maastricht">Maastricht</a> in 1579, killing over 10,000 civilians and thereby ensuring the rebellion continued.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In 1581, the northern provinces adopted the <a href="/wiki/Act_of_Abjuration" title="Act of Abjuration">Act of Abjuration</a>, the declaration of independence in which the provinces officially deposed Philip II as reigning monarch in the northern provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Against the rebels Philip could draw on the resources of the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a>, including in Iberia, Spanish America, Spanish Italy, and the Spanish Netherlands. Queen Elizabeth I of England sympathised with the Dutch struggle against England's Spanish rival and sent an army of 7,600 soldiers to aid the Dutch in their war with the Catholic Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> English forces under the Earl of Leicester and then Lord Willoughby faced the Spanish in the Netherlands under the <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Farnese,_Duke_of_Parma" title="Alexander Farnese, Duke of Parma">Duke of Parma</a> in a series of largely indecisive actions that tied down significant numbers of Spanish troops and bought time for the Dutch to reorganise their defences.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The war continued until 1648, when Spain under King <a href="/wiki/Philip_IV_of_Spain" title="Philip IV of Spain">Philip IV</a> finally recognised the independence of the seven north-western provinces in the <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_M%C3%BCnster" title="Peace of Münster">Peace of Münster</a>. Parts of the southern provinces became <i>de facto</i> colonies of the new republican-mercantile empire.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Dutch_Republic_.281581.E2.80.931795.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Dutch_Republic_(1581–1795)">Dutch Republic (1581–1795)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Evolution_of_the_Dutch_Empire" title="Evolution of the Dutch Empire">Evolution of the Dutch Empire</a></div> 	
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg/220px-De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="140" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg/330px-De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg/440px-De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="5972" data-file-height="3808" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:De_handelsloge_van_de_VOC_in_Hougly_in_Bengalen_Rijksmuseum_SK-A-4282.jpeg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company" title="Dutch East India Company">Dutch East India Company</a> factory in <a href="/wiki/Hugli-Chuchura" title="Hugli-Chuchura">Hugli-Chuchura</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mughal_Bengal" class="mw-redirect" title="Mughal Bengal">Mughal Bengal</a> by Hendrik van Schuylenburgh, 1665</div></div></div>
<p>After declaring their independence, the provinces of <a href="/wiki/County_of_Holland" title="County of Holland">Holland</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_of_Zeeland" title="County of Zeeland">Zeeland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lordship_of_Groningen" title="Lordship of Groningen">Groningen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lordship_of_Friesland" class="mw-redirect" title="Lordship of Friesland">Friesland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lordship_of_Utrecht" title="Lordship of Utrecht">Utrecht</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lordship_of_Overijssel" title="Lordship of Overijssel">Overijssel</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Gelderland" class="mw-redirect" title="Duchy of Gelderland">Gelderland</a> formed a <a href="/wiki/Confederation" title="Confederation">confederation</a>. All these duchies, lordships and counties were autonomous and had their own government, the <a href="/wiki/States-Provincial" class="mw-redirect" title="States-Provincial">States-Provincial</a>. The <a href="/wiki/States_General_of_the_Netherlands" title="States General of the Netherlands">States General</a>, the confederal government, were seated in <a href="/wiki/The_Hague" title="The Hague">The Hague</a> and consisted of representatives from each of the seven provinces. The sparsely populated region of <a href="/wiki/Lordship_of_Drenthe" class="mw-redirect" title="Lordship of Drenthe">Drenthe</a> was part of the republic too, although it was not considered one of the provinces. Moreover, the Republic had come to occupy during the <a href="/wiki/Eighty_Years%27_War" title="Eighty Years&#39; War">Eighty Years' War</a> a number of so-called <a href="/wiki/Generality_Lands" title="Generality Lands">Generality Lands</a> in <a href="/wiki/County_of_Flanders" title="County of Flanders">Flanders</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Brabant" title="Duchy of Brabant">Brabant</a> and <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Limburg" title="Duchy of Limburg">Limburg</a>. Their population was mainly Roman Catholic, and these areas did not have a governmental structure of their own and were used as a buffer zone between the Republic and the Spanish-controlled <a href="/wiki/Southern_Netherlands" title="Southern Netherlands">Southern Netherlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
